I don't like the green ones, they taste weird, kind of like copper or battery acid. \n\nBut I also started moving the boxes in these strange little patterns. I just arrange them in the patterns, even when everyone is yelling for me to stop. My supervisor yells a lot. He has this really loud voice, kind of like my uncle does when he watches football on Thanksgiving... he always gets so mad at the TV... but yeah, they brought me here because of the boxes and the freezing, I think.\n\nDoctor: When you taste that copper or battery acid flavor in the green energy drinks, or when you hear the forklift beeping — are these things actually happening around you, or do you sometimes experience these tastes and sounds when nothing is there?\n\nPatient: No, I mean... the forklift is definitely right there. It's real. I hear it because it's backing up in aisle four. Aisle four is where they put all the heavy pallets. And the energy drinks, I only taste that if I actually take a sip from someone's can. I don't just taste it out of nowhere. \n\nThe cans are aluminum, but the taste is... yeah, like copper. Copper is a really good conductor of electricity, actually. I learned about that in my physics class before I stopped going. That's why they use it in all the house wiring instead of other metals. My dad tried to rewire our kitchen light once and he blew the main circuit breaker. Sparks went everywhere, it was really bright. We had to eat cold sandwiches for like three days because the fridge got completely shut off and all the meat was going bad. I like turkey sandwiches okay, but only if the bread isn't soggy. When the tomatoes sit on the bread for too long in the lunchbox, it gets all mushy, and I just can't stand the texture of mushy bread. It feels wrong on my teeth.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned freezing and copying the forklift sounds and hand signals — when you do these things, are you aware that you're doing them, or do you sometimes find yourself doing them without realizing it?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure... I don't know if I realize it right when it happens. I know I do the hand signals, but... hand signals are super important, you know? Like if your turn signal breaks on your car. My buddy had this old Honda Civic, and the left blinker just completely died on him. We were driving down to the lake and he had to stick his arm out the window every time we turned. It was so hot that day, and his AC was totally busted too. We had to stop at this gas station to get ice cream just to cool down, but it melted all over my hands before we even got back to the car. I usually get strawberry, but they only had vanilla. Vanilla is kind of boring, it's like... the default flavor. It's like the regular flat cardboard of ice cream, just plain. But yeah, with the freezing at the warehouse... I don't really know. I just do it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up and do those hand signals and copying sounds at work, can you stop yourself if you really try to, or does it feel like something you can't control even if you wanted to stop?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really think I can stop. Like I was saying before, I just find myself doing it... freezing with my arms up by the forklift, making the beeping noises and the signals, or putting the boxes into those strange little patterns. Even when everyone is standing right there yelling at me to stop, I just keep doing it. I don't know if I can control it. \n\nMy supervisor yells so loud, it echoes off the corrugated metal roof. Metal roofs are crazy loud when it rains, actually. We had a tin roof on a shed growing up, and during a storm it sounded like a massive drum solo. My friend tried to learn the drums back in high school, but his parents made him put the drum kit out in the unheated garage because the cymbals gave his mom terrible migraines. Migraines sound awful, like your vision gets all blurry and your whole head pulses. I don't really get headaches much, mostly I just get thirsty from all the dust at work. The cardboard dust gets everywhere, it coats your throat and makes you cough. But yeah... with the freezing and the boxes... I don't think I can stop it. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these freezing episodes, the copying of sounds and hand signals, and the unusual box patterns at work? Has this been going on for just a few days or weeks, or has it been happening for several months or longer?\n\nPatient: I guess... it's been about six months now? Yeah, the weird stuff at the warehouse, the talking and the freezing up and everything... it's been going on for like six months. Six months is exactly half a year, which is how long I had to wait to get my driver's license after I failed the written test the first time. I didn't study the section on right-of-way at four-way stops because I thought it was obvious, but they try to trick you with the wording on those tests. The DMV is always so crowded, too. You have to take a little paper ticket with a number on it and just sit in those hard plastic chairs for hours. The chairs are always that weird shade of blue, like... hospital blue, you know? It's the same color as the scrubs the nurses wear when you go in for a flu shot. I hate needles, they make my stomach feel all tight and weird. But yeah, it's been about six months at work with all that stuff happening.\n\nDoctor: Before these six months started, did you ever have experiences like this — where you'd freeze up, copy sounds and movements, or arrange things in strange patterns — or is this completely new and different from how you've always been?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I've never really done anything like that before. It's completely new. Like, I've always just been normal, you know? Just going to work and doing my thing. I used to work at this pizza place before the warehouse, actually. We had to fold so many boxes there too, but they were different boxes. Pizza boxes are flat and you have to fold the edges in so they lock together. It's really satisfying when you get into a rhythm with it. I used to see how fast I could fold a stack of fifty. My manager there, he was this guy named Tony, and he always smelled like oregano and old cigarettes. He used to smoke out back by the dumpsters, and the seagulls would always circle around him because they knew he'd throw them crusts. Seagulls are crazy, they'll eat literally anything. I saw one swallow a whole French fry once without even chewing. But yeah, no, I never used to freeze up or do the copying stuff before all this started. It's just recent.\n\nDoctor: During these past six months, have you noticed yourself thinking that certain things — like the patterns in the boxes, or the sounds, or things people do around you — have special meanings or are connected to each other in ways that others don't seem to notice or understand?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think they have special meanings or anything. I just do it. I don't know why everyone makes such a big deal out of it, honestly. It's just boxes. I mean, boxes are everywhere when you really think about it. Everything comes in a box. When I moved into my apartment, I had to find so many boxes, and I went to the grocery store to ask for the ones they use for bananas. Banana boxes are the best because they have those thick bottoms and the handles on the sides, so they don't break when you put heavy stuff like books in them. I have a lot of books from when I was taking classes at the community college. Textbooks are ridiculously expensive, man. I spent like two hundred dollars on a biology book and we only used three chapters the whole semester. The professor was this older guy who always wore sweater vests, even when it was hot outside. I don't know how he didn't sweat to death. Sweating makes my skin itch, especially under my work shirt. But yeah, no... no special meanings to the patterns or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you want to talk or answer someone, but you find that you just can't speak at all — like your voice won't come out — even though there's nothing physically wrong with your throat?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think my voice ever just stops working like that. I can always talk if I want to. I just don't always want to, you know? Sometimes people just talk too much. Like my neighbor, he's always out on his porch when I get home, and he just goes on and on about his lawnmower. He has this riding mower, one of those zero-turn ones, and he treats it like it's a sports car or something. He's always waxing it and checking the oil. I don't even have a lawn, I live on the second floor. The stairs up to my place are made of that metal grating, the kind where you can see straight through to the ground when you walk up them. It always makes me feel a little dizzy if I look down. Heights are weird like that. I went to the top of a lighthouse once when I was a kid, on a field trip, and the wind was blowing so hard I thought the whole thing was going to tip over into the ocean. The ocean smells so salty when the wind picks up. But yeah, no, my voice works fine, I don't lose it or anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work or around other people, do you ever find yourself not wanting to talk much, or do you tend to keep talking and talking even when people seem like they want you to stop?\n\nPatient: No, I... I just talk when I need to. I don't really think about it, you know? Sometimes I just don't have much to say. But people always want you to talk. Like at the warehouse, they want you to be all friendly in the breakroom. The breakroom has these vending machines that are always broken. Last week, I put a dollar in for a bag of those spicy chips, and the coil just spun halfway and stopped. The chips were just hanging there. I tried shaking the machine, but it's bolted to the wall or something, it wouldn't budge. My buddy from community college used to know a trick to get free sodas out of those machines, you had to hit a certain sequence of buttons, like a cheat code in a video game. I used to play a lot of video games before I dropped out. Mostly those big open-world ones where you can just walk around for hours and look at the trees. The graphics on trees these days are insane, you can see every individual leaf. But yeah, I don't know, I just talk a normal amount, I guess. I don't really keep talking if people want me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations — like at work, or around certain people, or in particular places — where you feel like you can't speak, even though you want to communicate with someone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have that happen. I can talk if I want to, it's not like I'm stuck or anything. I just... I don't know, sometimes I just don't feel like it, especially at work. Work is just so loud anyway, with all the forklifts beeping. They have those backup alarms that just pierce right through your ears, beep, beep, beep, all shift long. My supervisor, Dave, he drives one of them, and he's always zipping around corners way too fast. One time he almost knocked over a whole pallet of dog food. Dog food bags are so heavy, man, like forty pounds each, and if they rip, it's just little brown pellets everywhere. My aunt used to have this dog, a golden retriever, and it would eat anything. It ate a whole sock once and had to go to the vet. Vets are so expensive. It's crazy how much it costs just to take an animal to the doctor, sometimes it feels like it's more than for a person. But no, I can speak fine anywhere, I don't get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up with your arms in the air by the forklift, does your body stay stiff in that exact position for a while, or can you move your arms and body around freely even while you're frozen?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know if I'm stiff. I guess they brought me in because I keep doing things like that at the warehouse. Like freezing with my arms up by the forklift, and I'll copy the beeping sounds it makes, or the hand signals the drivers do. Or I'll just be moving boxes in these strange little patterns, even when everyone is yelling for me to stop. They yell a lot at the warehouse. The acoustics in there are terrible, it's all concrete and metal shelves that go up to the ceiling. The ceiling has those big industrial lights that take forever to turn on, they hum when they warm up. It sounds like a giant swarm of bees. I got stung by a bee once when I was little, right on the eyelid. My whole face swelled up like a balloon, I couldn't even see out of that eye for two days. Ice didn't help at all. But yeah, I guess I just do those things by the forklift and the boxes, and they get mad.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like people at work or elsewhere were talking about you, or that things happening around you were directed at you or had to do with you specifically?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really think they're talking about me. I mean, they mostly just talk about the shift schedule or whatever sports game was on TV. I don't really watch sports, except maybe basketball sometimes. I used to shoot hoops at the park near my old apartment before I moved. The nets there were all torn up, so the city replaced them with those metal chain ones. When the basketball goes through a chain net, it makes this really cool metallic *shing* sound, you know? It sounds exactly like a sword being pulled out of a sheath in a movie. I watch a lot of action movies on my days off. There was this one I saw last week where a guy fought a whole army by himself in the pouring rain, and the rain effects were just amazing. I always wonder how they make fake rain on movie sets. They probably use these massive sprinklers attached to construction cranes or something. Cranes are crazy, just thinking about how they balance all that weight without tipping over. Physics is pretty wild like that. But yeah, no, I don't notice anyone talking about me specifically.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up with your arms in the air, if someone tried to gently move your arm down or change the position you're holding, would your arm stay in whatever new position they put it in, or would it go back to the position you had it in before?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't think anyone has ever tried to grab my arm and move it when I'm like that. Dave usually just yells from the forklift. He did grab my shoulder once, though, but that was just because I had my earplugs in too deep and couldn't hear him. We have to wear these neon orange foam earplugs because of OSHA rules. They come in these huge dispenser boxes on the wall in the breakroom, and you just turn the little knob and a handful falls out. It's exactly like those candy machines at the grocery store. I used to always beg my mom for a quarter to get Skittles from those machines when I was a kid. The red ones are the best, but sometimes you'd get a bunch of the yellow lemon ones, and those just taste like cleaning supplies. We use this industrial floor cleaner at work that smells exactly like artificial lemons, actually. It gives me a massive headache if I breathe it in too long. But yeah, I'm not sure if my arm would stay or go back. Nobody's ever touched me to find out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I... I really don't know. Like I said, nobody has ever tried to move me when I'm like that. I guess I just don't know what my arm would do. It's weird to think about someone just coming up and moving my arm like a mannequin. I saw this really creepy movie once about mannequins coming to life in a department store after hours. They had these stiff, jerky movements, like old stop-motion animation. Stop-motion is so tedious to make, you have to move the figures just a tiny fraction of an inch for every single frame. I tried making a claymation video for a school project in middle school, but the clay kept getting too warm from the desk lamp and melting. It was supposed to be a dinosaur, but it ended up looking like a green blob. Dinosaurs are fascinating, though. The fact that birds are basically modern-day dinosaurs is just wild. Like, a pigeon is related to a T-Rex. But yeah, I don't know if my arm would stay or go back.

Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic? What's been concerning you?\n\nPatient: I guess... I guess they brought me in because of the warehouse. I learned about materials a bit in college before I left to start working. Six months is exactly half a year, which is how long I had to wait to get my driver's license after I failed the written test the first time. I didn't study the section on right-of-way at four-way stops because I thought it was obvious, but they try to trick you with the wording on those tests. The DMV is always so crowded, too. You have to take a little paper ticket with a number on it and just sit in those hard plastic chairs for hours. The chairs are always that weird shade of blue, like... hospital blue, you know? It's the same color as the scrubs the nurses wear when you go in for a flu shot. I hate needles, they make my stomach feel all tight and weird. But yeah, it's been about six months at work with all that stuff happening.\n\nDoctor: Before these six months started, did you ever have experiences like this — where you'd freeze up, copy sounds and movements, or arrange things in strange patterns — or is this completely new and different from how you've always been?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I've never really done anything like that before. It's completely new. Like, I've always just been normal, you know? Just going to work and doing my thing. I used to work at this pizza place before the warehouse, actually. We had to fold so many boxes there too, but they were different boxes. Pizza boxes are flat and you have to fold the edges in so they lock together. It's really satisfying when you get into a rhythm with it. I used to see how fast I could fold a stack of fifty. My manager there, he was this guy named Tony, and he always smelled like oregano and old cigarettes. He used to smoke out back by the dumpsters, and the seagulls would always circle around him because they knew he'd throw them crusts. Seagulls are crazy, they'll eat literally anything. I saw one swallow a whole French fry once without even chewing. But yeah, no, I never used to freeze up or do the copying stuff before all this started. It's just recent.\n\nDoctor: During these past six months, have you noticed yourself thinking that certain things — like the patterns in the boxes, or the sounds, or things people do around you — have special meanings or are connected to each other in ways that others don't seem to notice or understand?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think they have special meanings or anything. I just do it. I don't know why everyone makes such a big deal out of it, honestly. It's just boxes. I mean, boxes are everywhere when you really think about it. Everything comes in a box. When I moved into my apartment, I had to find so many boxes, and I went to the grocery store to ask for the ones they use for bananas. Banana boxes are the best because they have those thick bottoms and the handles on the sides, so they don't break when you put heavy stuff like books in them. I have a lot of books from when I was taking classes at the community college. Textbooks are ridiculously expensive, man. I spent like two hundred dollars on a biology book and we only used three chapters the whole semester. The professor was this older guy who always wore sweater vests, even when it was hot outside. I don't know how he didn't sweat to death. Sweating makes my skin itch, especially under my work shirt. But yeah, no... no special meanings to the patterns or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you want to talk or answer someone, but you find that you just can't speak at all — like your voice won't come out — even though there's nothing physically wrong with your throat?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think my voice ever just stops working like that. I can always talk if I want to. I just don't always want to, you know? Sometimes people just talk too much. Like my neighbor, he's always out on his porch when I get home, and he just goes on and on about his lawnmower. He has this riding mower, one of those zero-turn ones, and he treats it like it's a sports car or something. He's always waxing it and checking the oil. I don't even have a lawn, I live on the second floor. The stairs up to my place are made of that metal grating, the kind where you can see straight through to the ground when you walk up them. It always makes me feel a little dizzy if I look down. Heights are weird like that. I went to the top of a lighthouse once when I was a kid, on a field trip, and the wind was blowing so hard I thought the whole thing was going to tip over into the ocean. The ocean smells so salty when the wind picks up. But yeah, no, my voice works fine, I don't lose it or anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work or around other people, do you ever find yourself not wanting to talk much, or do you tend to keep talking and talking even when people seem like they want you to stop?\n\nPatient: No, I... I just talk when I need to. I don't really think about it, you know? Sometimes I just don't have much to say. But people always want you to talk. Like at the warehouse, they want you to be all friendly in the breakroom. The breakroom has these vending machines that are always broken. Last week, I put a dollar in for a bag of those spicy chips, and the coil just spun halfway and stopped. The chips were just hanging there. I tried shaking the machine, but it's bolted to the wall or something, it wouldn't budge. My buddy from community college used to know a trick to get free sodas out of those machines, you had to hit a certain sequence of buttons, like a cheat code in a video game. I used to play a lot of video games before I dropped out. Mostly those big open-world ones where you can just walk around for hours and look at the trees. The graphics on trees these days are insane, you can see every individual leaf. But yeah, I don't know, I just talk a normal amount, I guess. I don't really keep talking if people want me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations — like at work, or around certain people, or in particular places — where you feel like you can't speak, even though you want to communicate with someone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have that happen. I can talk if I want to, it's not like I'm stuck or anything. I just... I don't know, sometimes I just don't feel like it, especially at work. Work is just so loud anyway, with all the forklifts beeping. They have those backup alarms that just pierce right through your ears, beep, beep, beep, all shift long. My supervisor, Dave, he drives one of them, and he's always zipping around corners way too fast. One time he almost knocked over a whole pallet of dog food. Dog food bags are so heavy, man, like forty pounds each, and if they rip, it's just little brown pellets everywhere. My aunt used to have this dog, a golden retriever, and it would eat anything. It ate a whole sock once and had to go to the vet. Vets are so expensive. It's crazy how much it costs just to take an animal to the doctor, sometimes it feels like it's more than for a person. But no, I can speak fine anywhere, I don't get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up with your arms in the air by the forklift, does your body stay stiff in that exact position for a while, or can you move your arms and body around freely even while you're frozen?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know if I'm stiff. I guess they brought me in because I keep doing things like that at the warehouse. Like freezing with my arms up by the forklift, and I'll copy the beeping sounds it makes, or the hand signals the drivers do. Or I'll just be moving boxes in these strange little patterns, even when everyone is yelling for me to stop. They yell a lot at the warehouse. The acoustics in there are terrible, it's all concrete and metal shelves that go up to the ceiling. The ceiling has those big industrial lights that take forever to turn on, they hum when they warm up. It sounds like a giant swarm of bees. I got stung by a bee once when I was little, right on the eyelid. My whole face swelled up like a balloon, I couldn't even see out of that eye for two days. Ice didn't help at all. But yeah, I guess I just do those things by the forklift and the boxes, and they get mad.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like people at work or elsewhere were talking about you, or that things happening around you were directed at you or had to do with you specifically?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really think they're talking about me. I mean, they mostly just talk about the shift schedule or whatever sports game was on TV. I don't really watch sports, except maybe basketball sometimes. I used to shoot hoops at the park near my old apartment before I moved. The nets there were all torn up, so the city replaced them with those metal chain ones. When the basketball goes through a chain net, it makes this really cool metallic *shing* sound, you know? It sounds exactly like a sword being pulled out of a sheath in a movie. I watch a lot of action movies on my days off. There was this one I saw last week where a guy fought a whole army by himself in the pouring rain, and the rain effects were just amazing. I always wonder how they make fake rain on movie sets. They probably use these massive sprinklers attached to construction cranes or something. Cranes are crazy, just thinking about how they balance all that weight without tipping over. Physics is pretty wild like that. But yeah, no, I don't notice anyone talking about me specifically.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up with your arms in the air, if someone tried to gently move your arm down or change the position you're holding, would your arm stay in whatever new position they put it in, or would it go back to the position you had it in before?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't think anyone has ever tried to grab my arm and move it when I'm like that. Dave usually just yells from the forklift. He did grab my shoulder once, though, but that was just because I had my earplugs in too deep and couldn't hear him. We have to wear these neon orange foam earplugs because of OSHA rules. They come in these huge dispenser boxes on the wall in the breakroom, and you just turn the little knob and a handful falls out. It's exactly like those candy machines at the grocery store. I used to always beg my mom for a quarter to get Skittles from those machines when I was a kid. The red ones are the best, but sometimes you'd get a bunch of the yellow lemon ones, and those just taste like cleaning supplies. We use this industrial floor cleaner at work that smells exactly like artificial lemons, actually. It gives me a massive headache if I breathe it in too long. But yeah, I'm not sure if my arm would stay or go back. Nobody's ever touched me to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're frozen with your arms up in the air, if I were to gently push your arm down into a different position, do you think your arm would stay down in that new position I put it in, or would it drift back up to where it was before?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know. Like I said, nobody has ever tried to move me when I'm like that. I guess I just don't know what my arm would do. It's weird to think about someone just coming up and moving my arm like a mannequin. I saw this really creepy movie once about mannequins coming to life in a department store after hours. They had these stiff, jerky movements, like old stop-motion animation. Stop-motion is so tedious to make, you have to move the figures just a tiny fraction of an inch for every single frame. I tried making a claymation video for a school project in middle school, but the clay kept getting too warm from the desk lamp and melting. It was supposed to be a dinosaur, but it ended up looking like a green blob. Dinosaurs are fascinating, though. The fact that birds are basically modern-day dinosaurs is just wild. Like, a pigeon is related to a T-Rex. But yeah, I don't know if my arm would stay or go back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  "candidates": ["F20.0", "F20.1"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

I got stung by a bee once when I was little, right on the eyelid. My whole face swelled up like a balloon, I couldn't even see out of that eye for two days. Ice didn't help at all. But yeah, I guess I just do those things by the forklift and the boxes, and they get mad.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like people at work or elsewhere were talking about you, or that things happening around you were directed at you or had to do with you specifically?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really think they're talking about me. I mean, they mostly just talk about the shift schedule or whatever sports game was on TV. I don't really watch sports, except maybe basketball sometimes. I used to shoot hoops at the park near my old apartment before I moved. The nets there were all torn up, so the city replaced them with those metal chain ones. When the basketball goes through a chain net, it makes this really cool metallic *shing* sound, you know? It sounds exactly like a sword being pulled out of a sheath in a movie. I watch a lot of action movies on my days off. There was this one I saw last week where a guy fought a whole army by himself in the pouring rain, and the rain effects were just amazing. I always wonder how they make fake rain on movie sets. They probably use these massive sprinklers attached to construction cranes or something. Cranes are crazy, just thinking about how they balance all that weight without tipping over. Physics is pretty wild like that. But yeah, no, I don't notice anyone talking about me specifically.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up with your arms in the air, if someone tried to gently move your arm down or change the position you're holding, would your arm stay in whatever new position they put it in, or would it go back to the position you had it in before?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't think anyone has ever tried to grab my arm and move it when I'm like that. Dave usually just yells from the forklift. They come in these huge dispenser boxes on the wall in the breakroom, and you just turn the little knob and a handful falls out. It's exactly like those candy machines at the grocery store. I used to always beg my mom for a quarter to get Skittles from those machines when I was a kid. The red ones are the best, but sometimes you'd get a bunch of the yellow lemon ones, and those just taste like cleaning supplies. We use this industrial floor cleaner at work that smells exactly like artificial lemons, actually. It gives me a massive headache if I breathe it in too long. But yeah, I'm not sure if my arm would stay or go back. Nobody's ever touched me to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're frozen with your arms up in the air, if I were to gently push your arm down into a different position, do you think your arm would stay down in that new position I put it in, or would it drift back up to where it was before?\n\nPatient: I... It's weird to think about someone just coming up and moving my arm like a mannequin. I saw this really creepy movie once about mannequins coming to life in a department store after hours. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F20.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic? What's been concerning you?\n\nPatient: I guess... I guess they brought me in because of the warehouse. I work freight, moving stock around. We get a lot of corrugated cardboard. Did you know there's a difference between regular cardboard and corrugated fiberboard? I learned about materials a bit in college before I left to start working. The fiberboard has that wavy layer inside so it can hold more weight. \n\nBut yeah, at work, I keep doing things... like, I'll just freeze. I just stop and hold my arms up in the air right by the forklift. And I copy the beeping sounds it makes when it backs up, and the hand signals the drivers use. The drivers are always drinking those huge energy drinks, the green cans. I don't like the green ones, they taste weird, kind of like copper or battery acid. \n\nBut I also started moving the boxes in these strange little patterns. I just arrange them in the patterns, even when everyone is yelling for me to stop. My supervisor yells a lot. He has this really loud voice, kind of like my uncle does when he watches football on Thanksgiving... he always gets so mad at the TV... but yeah, they brought me here because of the boxes and the freezing, I think.\n\nDoctor: When you taste that copper or battery acid flavor in the green energy drinks, or when you hear the forklift beeping — are these things actually happening around you, or do you sometimes experience these tastes and sounds when nothing is there?\n\nPatient: No, I mean... the forklift is definitely right there. It's real. I hear it because it's backing up in aisle four. Aisle four is where they put all the heavy pallets. And the energy drinks, I only taste that if I actually take a sip from someone's can. I don't just taste it out of nowhere. \n\nThe cans are aluminum, but the taste is... yeah, like copper. Copper is a really good conductor of electricity, actually. I learned about that in my physics class before I stopped going. That's why they use it in all the house wiring instead of other metals. My dad tried to rewire our kitchen light once and he blew the main circuit breaker. Sparks went everywhere, it was really bright. We had to eat cold sandwiches for like three days because the fridge got completely shut off and all the meat was going bad. I like turkey sandwiches okay, but only if the bread isn't soggy. When the tomatoes sit on the bread for too long in the lunchbox, it gets all mushy, and I just can't stand the texture of mushy bread. It feels wrong on my teeth.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned freezing and copying the forklift sounds and hand signals — when you do these things, are you aware that you're doing them, or do you sometimes find yourself doing them without realizing it?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure... I don't know if I realize it right when it happens. I know I do the hand signals, but... hand signals are super important, you know? Like if your turn signal breaks on your car. My buddy had this old Honda Civic, and the left blinker just completely died on him. We were driving down to the lake and he had to stick his arm out the window every time we turned. It was so hot that day, and his AC was totally busted too. We had to stop at this gas station to get ice cream just to cool down, but it melted all over my hands before we even got back to the car. I usually get strawberry, but they only had vanilla. Vanilla is kind of boring, it's like... the default flavor. It's like the regular flat cardboard of ice cream, just plain. But yeah, with the freezing at the warehouse... I don't really know. I just do it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up and do those hand signals and copying sounds at work, can you stop yourself if you really try to, or does it feel like something you can't control even if you wanted to stop?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really think I can stop. Like I was saying before, I just find myself doing it... freezing with my arms up by the forklift, making the beeping noises and the signals, or putting the boxes into those strange little patterns. Even when everyone is standing right there yelling at me to stop, I just keep doing it. I don't know if I can control it. \n\nMy supervisor yells so loud, it echoes off the corrugated metal roof. Metal roofs are crazy loud when it rains, actually. We had a tin roof on a shed growing up, and during a storm it sounded like a massive drum solo. My friend tried to learn the drums back in high school, but his parents made him put the drum kit out in the unheated garage because the cymbals gave his mom terrible migraines. Migraines sound awful, like your vision gets all blurry and your whole head pulses. I don't really get headaches much, mostly I just get thirsty from all the dust at work. The cardboard dust gets everywhere, it coats your throat and makes you cough. But yeah... with the freezing and the boxes... I don't think I can stop it. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these freezing episodes, the copying of sounds and hand signals, and the unusual box patterns at work? Has this been going on for just a few days or weeks, or has it been happening for several months or longer?\n\nPatient: I guess... it's been about six months now? Yeah, the weird stuff at the warehouse, the talking and the freezing up and everything... it's been going on for like six months. Six months is exactly half a year, which is how long I had to wait to get my driver's license after I failed the written test the first time. I didn't study the section on right-of-way at four-way stops because I thought it was obvious, but they try to trick you with the wording on those tests. The DMV is always so crowded, too. You have to take a little paper ticket with a number on it and just sit in those hard plastic chairs for hours. The chairs are always that weird shade of blue, like... hospital blue, you know? It's the same color as the scrubs the nurses wear when you go in for a flu shot. I hate needles, they make my stomach feel all tight and weird. But yeah, it's been about six months at work with all that stuff happening.\n\nDoctor: Before these six months started, did you ever have experiences like this — where you'd freeze up, copy sounds and movements, or arrange things in strange patterns — or is this completely new and different from how you've always been?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I've never really done anything like that before. It's completely new. Like, I've always just been normal, you know? Just going to work and doing my thing. I used to work at this pizza place before the warehouse, actually. We had to fold so many boxes there too, but they were different boxes. Pizza boxes are flat and you have to fold the edges in so they lock together. It's really satisfying when you get into a rhythm with it. I used to see how fast I could fold a stack of fifty. My manager there, he was this guy named Tony, and he always smelled like oregano and old cigarettes. He used to smoke out back by the dumpsters, and the seagulls would always circle around him because they knew he'd throw them crusts. Seagulls are crazy, they'll eat literally anything. I saw one swallow a whole French fry once without even chewing. But yeah, no, I never used to freeze up or do the copying stuff before all this started. It's just recent.\n\nDoctor: During these past six months, have you noticed yourself thinking that certain things — like the patterns in the boxes, or the sounds, or things people do around you — have special meanings or are connected to each other in ways that others don't seem to notice or understand?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think they have special meanings or anything. I just do it. I don't know why everyone makes such a big deal out of it, honestly. It's just boxes. I mean, boxes are everywhere when you really think about it. Everything comes in a box. When I moved into my apartment, I had to find so many boxes, and I went to the grocery store to ask for the ones they use for bananas. Banana boxes are the best because they have those thick bottoms and the handles on the sides, so they don't break when you put heavy stuff like books in them. I have a lot of books from when I was taking classes at the community college. Textbooks are ridiculously expensive, man. I spent like two hundred dollars on a biology book and we only used three chapters the whole semester. The professor was this older guy who always wore sweater vests, even when it was hot outside. I don't know how he didn't sweat to death. Sweating makes my skin itch, especially under my work shirt. But yeah, no... no special meanings to the patterns or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you want to talk or answer someone, but you find that you just can't speak at all — like your voice won't come out — even though there's nothing physically wrong with your throat?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think my voice ever just stops working like that. I can always talk if I want to. I just don't always want to, you know? Sometimes people just talk too much. Like my neighbor, he's always out on his porch when I get home, and he just goes on and on about his lawnmower. He has this riding mower, one of those zero-turn ones, and he treats it like it's a sports car or something. He's always waxing it and checking the oil. I don't even have a lawn, I live on the second floor. The stairs up to my place are made of that metal grating, the kind where you can see straight through to the ground when you walk up them. It always makes me feel a little dizzy if I look down. Heights are weird like that. I went to the top of a lighthouse once when I was a kid, on a field trip, and the wind was blowing so hard I thought the whole thing was going to tip over into the ocean. The ocean smells so salty when the wind picks up. But yeah, no, my voice works fine, I don't lose it or anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work or around other people, do you ever find yourself not wanting to talk much, or do you tend to keep talking and talking even when people seem like they want you to stop?\n\nPatient: No, I... I just talk when I need to. I don't really think about it, you know? Sometimes I just don't have much to say. But people always want you to talk. Like at the warehouse, they want you to be all friendly in the breakroom. The breakroom has these vending machines that are always broken. Last week, I put a dollar in for a bag of those spicy chips, and the coil just spun halfway and stopped. The chips were just hanging there. I tried shaking the machine, but it's bolted to the wall or something, it wouldn't budge. My buddy from community college used to know a trick to get free sodas out of those machines, you had to hit a certain sequence of buttons, like a cheat code in a video game. I used to play a lot of video games before I dropped out. Mostly those big open-world ones where you can just walk around for hours and look at the trees. The graphics on trees these days are insane, you can see every individual leaf. But yeah, I don't know, I just talk a normal amount, I guess. I don't really keep talking if people want me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations — like at work, or around certain people, or in particular places — where you feel like you can't speak, even though you want to communicate with someone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have that happen. I can talk if I want to, it's not like I'm stuck or anything. I just... I don't know, sometimes I just don't feel like it, especially at work. Work is just so loud anyway, with all the forklifts beeping. They have those backup alarms that just pierce right through your ears, beep, beep, beep, all shift long. My supervisor, Dave, he drives one of them, and he's always zipping around corners way too fast. One time he almost knocked over a whole pallet of dog food. Dog food bags are so heavy, man, like forty pounds each, and if they rip, it's just little brown pellets everywhere. My aunt used to have this dog, a golden retriever, and it would eat anything. It ate a whole sock once and had to go to the vet. Vets are so expensive. It's crazy how much it costs just to take an animal to the doctor, sometimes it feels like it's more than for a person. But no, I can speak fine anywhere, I don't get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up with your arms in the air by the forklift, does your body stay stiff in that exact position for a while, or can you move your arms and body around freely even while you're frozen?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know if I'm stiff. I guess they brought me in because I keep doing things like that at the warehouse. Like freezing with my arms up by the forklift, and I'll copy the beeping sounds it makes, or the hand signals the drivers do. Or I'll just be moving boxes in these strange little patterns, even when everyone is yelling for me to stop. They yell a lot at the warehouse. The acoustics in there are terrible, it's all concrete and metal shelves that go up to the ceiling. The ceiling has those big industrial lights that take forever to turn on, they hum when they warm up. It sounds like a giant swarm of bees. I got stung by a bee once when I was little, right on the eyelid. My whole face swelled up like a balloon, I couldn't even see out of that eye for two days. Ice didn't help at all. But yeah, I guess I just do those things by the forklift and the boxes, and they get mad.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like people at work or elsewhere were talking about you, or that things happening around you were directed at you or had to do with you specifically?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really think they're talking about me. I mean, they mostly just talk about the shift schedule or whatever sports game was on TV. I don't really watch sports, except maybe basketball sometimes. I used to shoot hoops at the park near my old apartment before I moved. The nets there were all torn up, so the city replaced them with those metal chain ones. When the basketball goes through a chain net, it makes this really cool metallic *shing* sound, you know? It sounds exactly like a sword being pulled out of a sheath in a movie. I watch a lot of action movies on my days off. There was this one I saw last week where a guy fought a whole army by himself in the pouring rain, and the rain effects were just amazing. I always wonder how they make fake rain on movie sets. They probably use these massive sprinklers attached to construction cranes or something. Cranes are crazy, just thinking about how they balance all that weight without tipping over. Physics is pretty wild like that. But yeah, no, I don't notice anyone talking about me specifically.\n\nDoctor: When you freeze up with your arms in the air, if someone tried to gently move your arm down or change the position you're holding, would your arm stay in whatever new position they put it in, or would it go back to the position you had it in before?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't think anyone has ever tried to grab my arm and move it when I'm like that. Dave usually just yells from the forklift. He did grab my shoulder once, though, but that was just because I had my earplugs in too deep and couldn't hear him. We have to wear these neon orange foam earplugs because of OSHA rules. They come in these huge dispenser boxes on the wall in the breakroom, and you just turn the little knob and a handful falls out. It's exactly like those candy machines at the grocery store. I used to always beg my mom for a quarter to get Skittles from those machines when I was a kid. The red ones are the best, but sometimes you'd get a bunch of the yellow lemon ones, and those just taste like cleaning supplies. We use this industrial floor cleaner at work that smells exactly like artificial lemons, actually. It gives me a massive headache if I breathe it in too long. But yeah, I'm not sure if my arm would stay or go back. Nobody's ever touched me to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're frozen with your arms up in the air, if I were to gently push your arm down into a different position, do you think your arm would stay down in that new position I put it in, or would it drift back up to where it was before?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know. Like I said, nobody has ever tried to move me when I'm like that. I guess I just don't know what my arm would do. It's weird to think about someone just coming up and moving my arm like a mannequin. I saw this really creepy movie once about mannequins coming to life in a department store after hours. They had these stiff, jerky movements, like old stop-motion animation. Stop-motion is so tedious to make, you have to move the figures just a tiny fraction of an inch for every single frame. I tried making a claymation video for a school project in middle school, but the clay kept getting too warm from the desk lamp and melting. It was supposed to be a dinosaur, but it ended up looking like a green blob. Dinosaurs are fascinating, though. The fact that birds are basically modern-day dinosaurs is just wild. Like, a pigeon is related to a T-Rex. But yeah, I don't know if my arm would stay or go back.\n\nDoctor: If someone moved your arm down to your side while you were frozen like that, would your arm just stay down where they put it, or would you feel it pulling back up to where it was?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I really can't tell you. You keep asking, but I just don't know because nobody's ever tried it. It's just me and Dave in the warehouse most of the time, and he's not going to come over and start posing me like an action figure. I used to have this huge collection of action figures when I was little, mostly the ones from that superhero cartoon that used to be on Saturday mornings. The joints on them would always get super loose after a while, so if you tried to make them stand up, their legs would just give out and they'd flop over. I tried fixing one once with superglue, but I accidentally glued the knee joint completely solid. Superglue is dangerous stuff, honestly. I read this article online about a guy who accidentally glued his eye shut because he thought the tube was his eye drops. Can you even imagine? You'd have to go to the ER for that. I hate hospitals, the lighting is always so harsh and fluorescent. It gives everything this weird greenish tint, kind of like the lighting in our warehouse down in aisle seven where the bulbs are going bad. I keep telling the supervisor we need new bulbs, but he never orders them. So yeah, I have no idea what my arm would do. It's never happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

He did grab my shoulder once, though, but that was just because I had my earplugs in too deep and couldn't hear him. We have to wear these neon orange foam earplugs because of OSHA rules. They come in these huge dispenser boxes on the wall in the breakroom, and you just turn the little knob and a handful falls out. It's exactly like those candy machines at the grocery store. I used to always beg my mom for a quarter to get Skittles from those machines when I was a kid. The red ones are the best, but sometimes you'd get a bunch of the yellow lemon ones, and those just taste like cleaning supplies. We use this industrial floor cleaner at work that smells exactly like artificial lemons, actually. It gives me a massive headache if I breathe it in too long. But yeah, I'm not sure if my arm would stay or go back. Nobody's ever touched me to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're frozen with your arms up in the air, if I were to gently push your arm down into a different position, do you think your arm would stay down in that new position I put it in, or would it drift back up to where it was before?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know. Like I said, nobody has ever tried to move me when I'm like that. I guess I just don't know what my arm would do. It's weird to think about someone just coming up and moving my arm like a mannequin. I saw this really creepy movie once about mannequins coming to life in a department store after hours. They had these stiff, jerky movements, like old stop-motion animation. Stop-motion is so tedious to make, you have to move the figures just a tiny fraction of an inch for every single frame. I tried making a claymation video for a school project in middle school, but the clay kept getting too warm from the desk lamp and melting. It was supposed to be a dinosaur, but it ended up looking like a green blob. Dinosaurs are fascinating, though. The fact that birds are basically modern-day dinosaurs is just wild. Like, a pigeon is related to a T-Rex. But yeah, I don't know if my arm would stay or go back.\n\nDoctor: If someone moved your arm down to your side while you were frozen like that, would your arm just stay down where they put it, or would you feel it pulling back up to where it was?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I really can't tell you. You keep asking, but I just don't know because nobody's ever tried it. It's just me and Dave in the warehouse most of the time, and he's not going to come over and start posing me like an action figure. I used to have this huge collection of action figures when I was little, mostly the ones from that superhero cartoon that used to be on Saturday mornings. The joints on them would always get super loose after a while, so if you tried to make them stand up, their legs would just give out and they'd flop over. I tried fixing one once with superglue, but I accidentally glued the knee joint completely solid. Superglue is dangerous stuff, honestly. I read this article online about a guy who accidentally glued his eye shut because he thought the tube was his eye drops. Can you even imagine? You'd have to go to the ER for that. I hate hospitals, the lighting is always so harsh and fluorescent. It gives everything this weird greenish tint, kind of like the lighting in our warehouse down in aisle seven where the bulbs are going bad. I keep telling the supervisor we need new bulbs, but he never orders them. So yeah, I have no idea what my arm would do. It's never happened.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf you're frozen with your arms up in the air and I gently push your arm down, does it feel like your arm is resisting me, like there's tension or stiffness in the muscles, or does it move easily without any resistance?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don't really know about tension or stiffness, either. Like I said, no one's tried to move me. I guess that's why they brought me in here, though. At the warehouse, I keep doing things like freezing with my arms up by the forklift. Oats are supposed to be good for your cholesterol, but I don't think I have to worry about that yet since I'm only twenty. My grandpa had high cholesterol, though. He used to eat bacon every single morning. But yeah, I don't know if my arm would resist you or not. I'm just doing the patterns and the freezing.

The joints on them would always get super loose after a while, so if you tried to make them stand up, their legs would just give out and they'd flop over. I tried fixing one once with superglue, but I accidentally glued the knee joint completely solid. Superglue is dangerous stuff, honestly. I read this article online about a guy who accidentally glued his eye shut because he thought the tube was his eye drops. Can you even imagine? You'd have to go to the ER for that. I hate hospitals, the lighting is always so harsh and fluorescent. It gives everything this weird greenish tint, kind of like the lighting in our warehouse down in aisle seven where the bulbs are going bad. I keep telling the supervisor we need new bulbs, but he never orders them. So yeah, I have no idea what my arm would do. It's never happened.\n\nDoctor: If you're frozen with your arms up in the air and I gently push your arm down, does it feel like your arm is resisting me, like there's tension or stiffness in the muscles, or does it move easily without any resistance?\n\nPatient: I don't really know about tension or stiffness, either. Like I said, no one's tried to move me. I guess that's why they brought me in here, though. At the warehouse, I keep doing things like freezing with my arms up by the forklift. I'll just stand there and copy the beeping sounds it makes when it backs up, or I'll do the hand signals the spotters use, over and over. Sometimes I'll take the boxes and start moving them in these strange little patterns, even when everyone is yelling for me to stop. They just yell and yell. It's so loud in there anyway, with all the machinery and the conveyor belts. The belts have these rubber rollers that squeak constantly, like a pack of mice trapped in the walls. We had mice in my dorm room when I was taking classes at the community college. My roommate left a box of cereal open on top of the fridge, and they just went to town on it. It was that cereal with the little marshmallows shaped like stars and moons. I always ate the marshmallows first and left the oat pieces. Oats are supposed to be good for your cholesterol, but I don't think I have to worry about that yet since I'm only twenty. My grandpa had high cholesterol, though. He used to eat bacon every single morning. But yeah, I don't know if my arm would resist you or not. I'm just doing the patterns and the freezing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Pizza boxes are flat and you have to fold the edges in so they lock together. It's really satisfying when you get into a rhythm with it. I used to see how fast I could fold a stack of fifty. I just do it. I don't know why everyone makes such a big deal out of it, honestly. It's just boxes. I mean, boxes are everywhere when you really think about it. Everything comes in a box. When I moved into my apartment, I had to find so many boxes, and I went to the grocery store to ask for the ones they use for bananas. Banana boxes are the best because they have those thick bottoms and the handles on the sides, so they don't break when you put heavy stuff like books in them. I have a lot of books from when I was taking classes at the community college. Textbooks are ridiculously expensive, man. Pizza boxes are flat and you have to fold the edges in so they lock together. It's really satisfying when you get into a rhythm with it. I used to see how fast I could fold a stack of fifty. I guess they brought me in because I keep doing things like that at the warehouse. I don't really watch sports, except maybe basketball sometimes. I used to shoot hoops at the park near my old apartment before I moved. It sounds exactly like a sword being pulled out of a sheath in a movie. I watch a lot of action movies on my days off. They probably use these massive sprinklers attached to construction cranes or something. Cranes are crazy, just thinking about how they balance all that weight without tipping over. The red ones are the best, but sometimes you'd get a bunch of the yellow lemon ones, and those just taste like cleaning supplies. We use this industrial floor cleaner at work that smells exactly like artificial lemons, actually. It gives me a massive headache if I breathe it in too long. But yeah, I'm not sure if my arm would stay or go back. Nobody's ever touched me to find out.\n\nDoctor: When you're frozen with your arms up in the air, if I were to gently push your arm down into a different position, do you think your arm would stay down in that new position I put it in, or would it drift back up to where it was before?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know. Like I said, nobody has ever tried to move me when I'm like that. I guess I just don't know what my arm would do. It's weird to think about someone just coming up and moving my arm like a mannequin. I saw this really creepy movie once about mannequins coming to life in a department store after hours. They had these stiff, jerky movements, like old stop-motion animation. Stop-motion is so tedious to make, you have to move the figures just a tiny fraction of an inch for every single frame. I tried making a claymation video for a school project in middle school, but the clay kept getting too warm from the desk lamp and melting. It was supposed to be a dinosaur, but it ended up looking like a green blob. Dinosaurs are fascinating, though. The fact that birds are basically modern-day dinosaurs is just wild. Like, a pigeon is related to a T-Rex. But yeah, I don't know if my arm would stay or go back.\n\nDoctor: If someone moved your arm down to your side while you were frozen like that, would your arm just stay down where they put it, or would you feel it pulling back up to where it was?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I really can't tell you. You keep asking, but I just don't know because nobody's ever tried it. It's just me and Dave in the warehouse most of the time, and he's not going to come over and start posing me like an action figure. I used to have this huge collection of action figures when I was little, mostly the ones from that superhero cartoon that used to be on Saturday mornings. The joints on them would always get super loose after a while, so if you tried to make them stand up, their legs would just give out and they'd flop over. I tried fixing one once with superglue, but I accidentally glued the knee joint completely solid. Superglue is dangerous stuff, honestly. I read this article online about a guy who accidentally glued his eye shut because he thought the tube was his eye drops. Can you even imagine? You'd have to go to the ER for that. I hate hospitals, the lighting is always so harsh and fluorescent. It gives everything this weird greenish tint, kind of like the lighting in our warehouse down in aisle seven where the bulbs are going bad. I keep telling the supervisor we need new bulbs, but he never orders them. So yeah, I have no idea what my arm would do. It's never happened.\n\nDoctor: If you're frozen with your arms up in the air and I gently push your arm down, does it feel like your arm is resisting me, like there's tension or stiffness in the muscles, or does it move easily without any resistance?\n\nPatient: I don't really know about tension or stiffness, either. Like I said, no one's tried to move me. I guess that's why they brought me in here, though. At the warehouse, I keep doing things like freezing with my arms up by the forklift. I'll just stand there and copy the beeping sounds it makes when it backs up, or I'll do the hand signals the spotters use, over and over. Sometimes I'll take the boxes and start moving them in these strange little patterns, even when everyone is yelling for me to stop. They just yell and yell. It's so loud in there anyway, with all the machinery and the conveyor belts. The belts have these rubber rollers that squeak constantly, like a pack of mice trapped in the walls. We had mice in my dorm room when I was taking classes at the community college. My roommate left a box of cereal open on top of the fridge, and they just went to town on it. It was that cereal with the little marshmallows shaped like stars and moons. I always ate the marshmallows first and left the oat pieces. Oats are supposed to be good for your cholesterol, but I don't think I have to worry about that yet since I'm only twenty. My grandpa had high cholesterol, though. He used to eat bacon every single morning. But yeah, I don't know if my arm would resist you or not. I'm just doing the patterns and the freezing.\n\nDoctor: I'd like to try something with your permission. I'm going to gently lift your arm up right now. I don't really know, it just feels like my arm. I don't think it's heavy or resisting you. It's hard to tell what heavy even means anymore, honestly, because I'm so used to lifting massive things at work. We get these huge shipments of dog food bags, the fifty-pound ones, and you have to stack them perfectly on the pallets or the shrink wrap won't hold them. Shrink wrap is so weird, it's just this giant roll of cling film. I used to wrap my roommate's door in cling film as a prank back when I was taking classes at the community college. He got so mad because he was late for his astronomy class trying to cut through it with his keys. Astronomy is mind-blowing, though. Like, the fact that the light we see from stars is actually millions of years old, so we're basically looking into the past. Time travel is a cool concept, but I feel like I'd accidentally step on a bug and change the whole future, like in that one story we had to read in high school. But yeah, my arm just feels normal when you do that, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I think it just stays down. Yeah, it stays right where you put it. It doesn't pull back up or anything. It's kind of like... you know when you drop something heavy and it just hits the floor and stays there? Like one of those fifty-pound dog food bags I was talking about. If you drop one of those, it doesn't bounce, it just lands with this huge thud. Dust goes everywhere. The dust in the warehouse is terrible, honestly. It coats everything in this fine gray powder, even the forklift seats. You have to wipe them down every morning or your pants get ruined. I ruined my favorite pair of jeans that way. They were these dark blue Levi's that I got on sale at the mall. The mall is practically empty nowadays, though. All the good stores closed down, and now it's just, like, a random candle shop and a place that sells oversized pretzels. I love those pretzels, especially with the cheese dip, but they're so expensive now. Inflation is crazy. My grandpa used to talk about how he could buy a whole meal for a quarter when he was a kid. A quarter! You can't even get a piece of gum from a machine for a quarter anymore. The machines always eat your coin anyway. But yeah, my arm just stays down.

We get these huge shipments of dog food bags, the fifty-pound ones, and you have to stack them perfectly on the pallets or the shrink wrap won't hold them. Shrink wrap is so weird, it's just this giant roll of cling film. I used to wrap my roommate's door in cling film as a prank back when I was taking classes at the community college. If you drop one of those, it doesn't bounce, it just lands with this huge thud. Dust goes everywhere. The dust in the warehouse is terrible, honestly. It coats everything in this fine gray powder, even the forklift seats. You have to wipe them down every morning or your pants get ruined. I ruined my favorite pair of jeans that way. They were these dark blue Levi's that I got on sale at the mall. The mall is practically empty nowadays, though. All the good stores closed down, and now it's just, like, a random candle shop and a place that sells oversized pretzels. I love those pretzels, especially with the cheese dip, but they're so expensive now. Inflation is crazy. My grandpa used to talk about how he could buy a whole meal for a quarter when he was a kid.
